---
title: Performance
description: How xcc-compiled code compares with clang's Objective-C and C++, with GCC's C++ and with Swift on the same programs, measured on twenty benchmarks across arm64 and x86-64.
---

The compiler is measured on twenty programs, each written four times: in the
xc language, in Objective-C with ARC, in C++ and in Swift, doing the same work
with the same algorithm and the same data. All are built with optimisation
(`-O3` for xc, Objective-C and C++, `-O` for Swift), every version prints a
checksum, and a run only counts if all four checksums agree. Every program, in
all four languages, is on the [benchmark sources](/compiler/benchmark-sources/)
page; each benchmark's name below links to its own. On x86-64 the C++ source
is built twice, with clang and with GCC (`g++ -O3`), on both targets, so each
carries a column for each C++ compiler.

Each figure is measured with the released 0.8 `xcc` on two machines:

- **arm64:** an Apple MacBook Pro with an M4 Max (12 performance and 4
  efficiency cores), macOS; Apple clang 17.0.0, GCC 16.2.0 and Swift 6.2;
- **x86-64:** an AMD Ryzen 9 9955HX (16 cores, 32 threads), Linux; GCC 15.2.0, clang 21.1.8 and Swift 6.1.3; its
  CPU frequency governor is `performance` (amd-pstate-epp) for the run.

Every compiler is given the machine's full instruction set: `-march=native`
(x86-64) or `-mcpu=native` (arm64) for clang and GCC, `-target-cpu native` for
Swift. xc needs no flag for that: with its default `-msimd=auto`, xcc builds
each vectorised function for SSE2, AVX2 and AVX-512 and the program picks one
when it starts. Nothing else is set: no link-time optimisation, no
profile-guided build, no `-ffast-math`, on any side.

Times are seconds for the timed region, the best of five runs, each run waiting
until the machine is otherwise idle.

## Summary

How much faster xc's code runs than each language's, as the geometric mean
over nineteen of the twenty benchmarks:

| xc compared with | arm64 (Apple M4 Max) | x86-64 (AMD Ryzen 9 9955HX) |
|---|---|---|
| Objective-C | **1.41× faster** | **1.14× faster** |
| C++ (clang) | **1.09× faster** | **1.12× slower** |
| C++ (GCC) | **1.17× faster** | **1.05× faster** |
| Swift | **1.41× faster** | **1.31× faster** |

On arm64 xc is ahead of all of them. On x86-64 xc is ahead of Objective-C, C++ (GCC) and Swift and behind C++ (clang).

The twentieth benchmark, `matrix_mul_f32`, is kept out of those means. xcc
recognises its loop nest and replaces it with a matrix kernel of its own, which
makes xc tens to hundreds of times faster at that one operation; counted in, it would
dominate means that stand for ordinary code, and most programs do not multiply
matrices. Its figures are under [Matrix multiplies](#matrix-multiplies).

## Per benchmark

Each row is a benchmark, each dot one language: how much faster or slower xc
is than that language. Dots left of the centre line are benchmarks xc wins. Choose a
release above a chart to see how it stood then; the rows stay in 0.8's
order. Releases before 0.65 were measured against Objective-C alone, apart
from `matrix_mul_f32`, which was added later and measured against all three.

| [`array_sum`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#array_sum) | 0.82 | 0.90 | 0.90 | 3.21 | 0.92 | **1.10× faster** |
| [`sieve`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#sieve) | 1.05 | 1.45 | 1.47 | 1.30 | 1.99 | **1.24× faster** |
| [`string_scan`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#string_scan) | 0.94 | 2.37 | 2.42 | 1.43 | 2.44 | **1.52× faster** |
| [`matrix_mul_f32`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#matrix_mul_f32) | 0.03 | 9.73 | 9.78 | 9.73 | 9.74 | **322× faster** |

### x86-64

| benchmark | xc | Objective-C | C++ (clang) | C++ (GCC) | Swift | xc vs the fastest of the others |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`int_muldiv`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#int_muldiv) | 0.51 | 0.34 | 0.33 | 0.17 | 0.34 | **3.0× slower** |
| [`arc_array`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#arc_array) | 1.15 | 3.72 | 0.77 | 0.63 | 0.56 | **2.1× slower** |
| [`array_sum`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#array_sum) | 0.36 | 0.22 | 0.22 | 0.71 | 0.22 | **1.66× slower** |
| [`call_depth`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#call_depth) | 0.26 | 0.19 | 0.19 | 0.24 | 0.19 | **1.40× slower** |
| [`struct_copy`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#struct_copy) | 0.85 | 0.87 | 0.87 | 0.65 | 0.87 | **1.32× slower** |
| [`sieve`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#sieve) | 0.98 | 0.75 | 0.74 | 0.82 | 1.15 | **1.32× slower** |
| [`sort_small`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#sort_small) | 0.91 | 0.71 | 0.71 | 0.82 | 1.11 | **1.28× slower** |
| [`array_map`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#array_map) | 0.32 | 0.27 | 0.27 | 2.02 | 0.51 | **1.22× slower** |
| [`matrix_mul`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#matrix_mul) | 0.09 | 0.13 | 0.08 | 0.08 | 1.88 | **1.16× slower** |
| [`float_math`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#float_math) | 0.76 | 0.74 | 0.73 | 0.67 | 0.74 | **1.13× slower** |
| [`poly_dispatch`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#poly_dispatch) | 0.97 | 1.23 | 0.93 | 0.93 | 6.67 | **1.04× slower** |
| [`mem_copy`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#mem_copy) | 0.35 | 0.52 | 0.34 | 0.38 | 0.54 | **the same** |
| [`bit_ops`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#bit_ops) | 0.97 | 0.97 | 0.97 | 0.97 | 0.97 | **the same** |
| [`branch_mix`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#branch_mix) | 0.81 | 0.81 | 0.81 | 0.81 | 0.81 | **the same** |
| [`int_accum`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#int_accum) | 0.97 | 0.97 | 0.97 | 0.97 | 0.97 | **the same** |
| [`hash_mix`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#hash_mix) | 1.01 | 1.01 | 1.01 | 1.01 | 1.01 | **the same** |
| [`arc_alloc`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#arc_alloc) | 0.40 | 2.20 | 0.44 | 0.44 | 0.74 | **1.11× faster** |
| [`method_call`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#method_call) | 1.30 | 1.90 | 1.58 | 1.58 | 1.45 | **1.12× faster** |
| [`string_scan`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#string_scan) | 0.47 | 0.55 | 0.55 | 0.84 | 0.54 | **1.15× faster** |
| [`matrix_mul_f32`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#matrix_mul_f32) | 0.22 | 8.34 | 8.28 | 0.80 | 7.99 | **3.6× faster** |

The fastest results are where the runtime does the work: `string_scan`,
`method_call` and `struct_copy` are byte scanning, dynamic dispatch and
aggregate copies. The slowest show where xcc's code generation has most to
gain:

- **Vectorisation.** On arm64, `int_muldiv` and `float_math` are vectorised
  by both compilers, and clang's loops are tighter; `matrix_mul` has matched
  clang there since 0.73. On x86-64 `matrix_mul` is still behind, because
  clang keeps the second matrix's rows in registers across the outer loop.
- **Reference counting on x86-64.** `arc_array`, where C++ reads elements
  without retaining them.
- **x86-64 loops and calls.** `sieve` and `sort_small`, where clang's loops
  are still tighter (in 0.74 `sort_small` was 2.6× behind; 0.75 rotates its
  inner loop), `call_depth`, where clang's call sequence is shorter, and
  `int_muldiv`, where GCC hoists the arithmetic that does not depend on the
  repetition out of it.

## Matrix multiplies

A dense matrix multiply written as three plain loops, `C[i][j]` the sum over
`k` of `A[i][k] · B[k][j]` in `float` or `double`, runs as a kernel the
compiler writes itself (from 0.71). The results are the loops', to the last
bit, and `-fno-matmul` turns it off. The kernel depends on the machine:

- **Apple M4 and later (SME):** the multiply runs on the matrix unit. From
  0.72 it works on a 2×2 block of the unit's tiles at once, and under
  [`:goal(speed)`](/compiler/language/statements/#speed-or-accuracy-goal),
  which `matrix_mul_f32` uses, it leaves out a NaN check that only affects the
  bits of a NaN (the inputs have none).
- **x86-64:** the widest vector unit the processor has (SSE2, AVX2 or
  AVX-512), chosen when the program starts; the Ryzen 9 9955HX picks AVX-512.

`matrix_mul_f32` measures it: a 128×128 `float` multiply, 10,000 times.
(`matrix_mul` multiplies `u32` values, which the matrix unit does not take, so
it stays a vectorised loop.)

| `matrix_mul_f32` | xc | Objective-C | C++ (clang) | C++ (GCC) | Swift |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| arm64, Apple M4 Max | 30 ms | 9.7 s | 9.8 s | 9.7 s | 9.7 s |
| xc is | | **322× faster** | **323× faster** | **322× faster** | **322× faster** |
| x86-64, AMD Ryzen 9 9955HX | 224 ms | 8.3 s | 8.3 s | 802 ms | 8.0 s |
| xc is | | **37× faster** | **37× faster** | **3.6× faster** | **36× faster** |

## Parallel blocks and the GPU

A [`par` block](/compiler/language/par/) runs a loop's iterations at once,
across every CPU thread, on the GPU (from 0.7), or across several devices at
once (from 0.8): the block is cut into tiles handed to every GPU the machine
has, and to the CPU, together. The GPU is reached through:

- **Metal** on Apple silicon;
- **CUDA** on an NVIDIA GPU under Windows;
- **Vulkan** on Linux, Windows and Android (from 0.72);
- **WebGPU** in a browser (from 0.72).

Six programs in `benchmark/par` measure it:

- `mandelbrot`: a 2048×2048 escape-time image, at most 256 iterations a pixel;
- `perlin`: 2048×2048 improved noise, four octaves;
- `nbody`: the force on each of 8192 bodies from all the others;
- `saxpy`: `y = a·x + y` over 16 million integers, with a sum;
- `md5`, `md5_mask`: the hashcat MD5 workload, hashing 64 million candidates
  through the full 64-round compression and summing the digests.

Each runs its block eight times and reports the best run. The single-device
modes are *one thread* (`XC_PAR=cpu XC_PAR_THREADS=1`), *all threads*
(`XC_PAR=cpu`) and *GPU* (`XC_PAR=gpu`). From 0.8 a block can also run on *every
GPU* (`XC_PAR=gpus`) or on *every device* (`XC_PAR=all`), and `XC_PAR_WORKERS=…`
names an exact set (`cuda`, `vulkan`, `metal`, `cpu`). `auto`, the default,
times the sets the machine can form and keeps the fastest; from 0.73 it
remembers the choice, per program and per machine, so later runs do not measure
again. Every mode's checksum must agree. On Windows *GPU* is CUDA and *Vulkan*
the same work through Vulkan (`XC_PAR_GPU=vulkan`).

**Apple MacBook Pro, M4 Max (12 performance and 4 efficiency cores), its GPU through Metal** (ms; best of eight runs)

| benchmark | one thread | all threads | GPU | all GPUs | all devices | `auto` | GPU vs all threads | GPU's first run |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`mandelbrot`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#mandelbrot) | 490 | 94.4 | 0.7 | 0.8 | 42.7 | 0.6 | 126× faster | 22.0 |
| [`md5`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#md5) | 6878 | 575 | 4.1 | 4.8 | 265 | 4.1 | 140× faster | 38.3 |
| [`md5_mask`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#md5_mask) | 6070 | 460 | 3.5 | 3.6 | 214 | 3.5 | 131× faster | 23.3 |
| [`nbody`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#nbody) | 20.2 | 2.0 | 0.4 | 1.2 | 10.3 | 1.0 | 5.1× faster | 20.2 |
| [`perlin`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#perlin) | 81.9 | 9.0 | 1.3 | 1.4 | 5.9 | 1.3 | 6.7× faster | 30.2 |
| [`saxpy`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#saxpy) | 3.4 | 0.5 | 10.9 | 12.2 | 9.2 | 0.5 | 20× slower | 31.0 |

**AMD Ryzen 9 9955HX (16 cores, 32 threads), Linux, its integrated Radeon 610M (2 compute units, 128 shader lanes) through Vulkan** (ms; best of eight runs)

| benchmark | one thread | all threads | GPU | all GPUs | all devices | `auto` | GPU vs all threads | GPU's first run |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`mandelbrot`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#mandelbrot) | 395 | 40.7 | 9.7 | 9.7 | 21.6 | 9.7 | 4.2× faster | 29.7 |
| [`md5`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#md5) | 6408 | 249 | 108 | 110 | 149 | 108 | 2.3× faster | 135 |
| [`md5_mask`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#md5_mask) | 5143 | 207 | 89.1 | 87.6 | 109 | 88.8 | 2.3× faster | 114 |
| [`nbody`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#nbody) | 5.9 | 0.5 | 3.4 | 3.6 | 3.2 | 0.6 | 7.1× slower | 21.7 |
| [`perlin`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#perlin) | 299 | 17.0 | 5.8 | 5.8 | 11.7 | 5.8 | 2.9× faster | 30.3 |
| [`saxpy`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#saxpy) | 3.9 | 2.4 | 21.7 | 15.7 | 11.9 | 2.4 | 9.1× slower | 57.0 |

**AMD Ryzen 9 5950X (16 cores, 32 threads), Windows, an NVIDIA RTX 5080 through CUDA and an RTX 3090 through Vulkan** (ms; best of eight runs)

| benchmark | one thread | all threads | GPU | all GPUs | Vulkan | all devices | `auto` | GPU vs all threads | GPU's first run | Vulkan's first run |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`mandelbrot`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#mandelbrot) | 447 | 46.6 | 0.1 | 0.4 | 0.3 | 21.3 | 0.1 | 333× faster | 105 | 125 |
| [`md5`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#md5) | 6954 | 299 | 0.9 | 0.9 | 1.6 | 148 | 0.9 | 343× faster | 93.3 | 126 |
| [`md5_mask`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#md5_mask) | 5636 | 275 | 0.7 | 0.8 | 1.3 | 186 | 0.7 | 379× faster | 95.2 | 139 |
| [`nbody`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#nbody) | 142 | 5.3 | 0.4 | 0.4 | 0.5 | 0.4 | 0.4 | 13.3× faster | 97.8 | 127 |
| [`perlin`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#perlin) | 353 | 19.0 | 0.5 | 0.4 | 1.1 | 60.3 | 0.3 | 38× faster | 100 | 131 |
| [`saxpy`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#saxpy) | 5.2 | 3.2 | 15.9 | 7.8 | 30.4 | 3.3 | 3.4 | 5.1× slower | 110 | 160 |

**Chrome on the Apple M4 Max: wasm32 (one thread) and WebGPU (from 0.73)** (ms; best of eight runs)

| benchmark | one thread | all threads | GPU | `auto` | GPU vs one thread | GPU's first run |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`mandelbrot`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#mandelbrot) | 364 | – | 1.7 | 1.5 | 214× faster | 8.2 |
| [`md5`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#md5) | 4584 | – | 4.7 | 4.6 | 975× faster | 18.1 |
| [`md5_mask`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#md5_mask) | 3783 | – | 3.6 | 3.7 | 1051× faster | 16.0 |
| [`nbody`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#nbody) | 53.5 | – | 1.3 | 1.3 | 41× faster | 6.0 |
| [`perlin`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#perlin) | 90.8 | – | 2.3 | 2.3 | 39× faster | 8.4 |
| [`saxpy`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#saxpy) | 9.0 | – | 21.8 | 8.8 | 2.4× slower | 57.7 |

**Mixing graphics APIs on one machine.** The `all GPUs` column above runs both NVIDIA cards through CUDA. The scheduler does not require one API, though: a block can run the RTX 5080 through CUDA beside the RTX 3090 through Vulkan, through the same tile queue. On the Windows host (ms, best of eight runs):

| both cards share a block | `saxpy` (16M) | `md5` (64M) |
|---|---|---|
| through CUDA (the `all GPUs` column) | 7.8 | 0.90 |
| 5080 through CUDA, 3090 through Vulkan | 9.6 | 1.02 |

The mixed pair is a little slower here only because the 3090 is slower through Vulkan than through CUDA; the point is that it runs at all. Two cards from different vendors — an NVIDIA through CUDA and an AMD through Vulkan — could share a block the same way.


What the tables show:

- **A discrete GPU wins by a wide margin** on the programs that do real work per
  element — `md5` hashes 64 million candidates in about a millisecond on one
  discrete card against hundreds on the CPU — and `auto` finds that and uses it.
- **`saxpy` stays on the CPU.** It does one multiply-add for every eight bytes
  it moves, so copying to and from the GPU outweighs the arithmetic, and `auto`
  keeps it on the CPU everywhere.
- **The tile queue scales a block across GPUs, where that pays (from 0.8).**
  `saxpy` spreads across both cards as the sum of their PCIe links — one GPU
  16 ms, both 7.8 — though it is memory-bound, so the CPU still wins and `auto`
  keeps it there. `md5` at 64 million candidates is already 0.9 ms on one card,
  too small for a second to help: the queue's per-tile overhead cancels the
  compute it would save, both cards together are no faster, and `auto` keeps it
  on the single card. A second GPU pays on a block big enough to need its
  compute and not bound by the transfer to it.
- **Mixed graphics APIs (from 0.8).** The scheduler does not require one API: a
  block can run one card through CUDA beside another through Vulkan (`saxpy`
  9.6 ms, against 7.8 for both through CUDA). It works across APIs, so an NVIDIA
  and an AMD card could share a block the same way.
- **The Ryzen's integrated Radeon** has 128 shader lanes against the CPU's 32
  threads. It just beats them on `mandelbrot`, `perlin` and `md5` and loses on
  `nbody` and `saxpy`, where `auto` keeps the CPU.
- **Vulkan against CUDA:** the RTX 3090 through Vulkan against the RTX 5080 through CUDA, two cards, so this compares the APIs only roughly: Vulkan is slower on `mandelbrot` (0.3 ms against 0.1) and `md5` (1.6 ms against 0.9) and `md5_mask` (1.3 ms against 0.7) and `nbody` (0.5 ms against 0.4) and `perlin` (1.1 ms against 0.5) and `saxpy` (30.4 ms against 15.9).
- **The first run** carries one-off costs (building the kernel, and on NVIDIA
  creating the driver context and compiling the PTX or SPIR-V), so it is shown
  apart: a few tens of milliseconds on Metal and the integrated GPU, and on the
  3090 93 to 110 ms through CUDA and 125 to 160 ms through Vulkan. `auto` pays it
  once, while it measures.

## Release to release

How fast xc's code is in each release, relative to 0.62: above 1× is faster.
The thin lines are single benchmarks (labelled where they moved by more than
twelve percent), the thick line the geometric mean (without `matrix_mul_f32`). Releases shown: 0.62, 0.63, 0.64, 0.65, 0.66, 0.7, 0.71, 0.72, 0.73, 0.74, 0.75, 0.8. `arc_alloc`, `method_call` changed in
0.64's benchmark set and are left out of this history.

## What is being compared, and what is not

**The compiler that ships.** The xc numbers come from the `xcc` in the download.

**The same work in every language.** Every version keeps its data where the xc
version keeps it (local arrays, not `static` ones, which clang optimises
differently) and leaves nothing a compiler can remove: an object that could be
put on the stack outlives its iteration, a call whose target could be resolved
at compile time takes its class at run time, and results are folded in so no
loop has a closed form. Where the original's point is reference-counted
objects, the C++ version uses `std::shared_ptr` and the Swift version a class,
so they pay for reference counting too.

**Different runtimes on the two targets.** The Objective-C column is Apple's
Foundation on arm64 and GNUstep with libobjc2 on x86-64, and Swift is 6.2 on the
Mac and 6.1 on Linux. These are different implementations, so a language's
times compare within a target and not across one. Swift on Linux is markedly
slower on `poly_dispatch` and `array_map` than on the Mac; the runs were
repeated on an idle machine and reproduce.

**Timed regions of about one second.** Each benchmark times its own inner loop
rather than the process, so start-up and data set-up are excluded.

**Alignment noise on x86-64.** xcc aligns every loop head on x86-64 to a 32-byte
boundary and the start of `.text` to 64 bytes, so an unrelated change elsewhere
cannot move a loop across a fetch boundary; see
[Optimisation](/compiler/usage/optimization/#per-target-settings).

## Reproducing

The sources are in `benchmark/src`, one `.xc`, `.m`, `.cpp` and `.swift` per
program, and the runner builds and times them all:

```
python3 benchmark/run.py --opt O3 --repeats 5
python3 benchmark/page.py
```

The x86-64 legs cross-build xc here and build the other languages on the
configured Linux host; without one the runner measures this machine only.
`--langs` measures some of the languages and adds them to the results already
there. Results land in `benchmark/<version>/results.json`, and `page.py` turns
them into this page.
